Batch optimization for balanced binary sequences and DNA sequences
Authors: Yiming Ma
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
BackgroundDNA data storage offers exceptional density and longevity, but its practicality is hampered by the high cost and low throughput of de novo DNA synthesis. A key cost driver in array-based synthesis is the length of a common supersequence required to encode a batch of DNA strands.ObjectiveThis study aims to address this cost bottleneck by investigating the optimal batch partitioning of DNA sequences. Our goal is to minimize the total synthesis cost, which is defined as the sum of the lengths of the shortest common supersequences (SCS) across all batches.ResultsGiven a large pool \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} of balanced binary sequences, which is partitioned into k batches with almost equal size, we define the total cost of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} to be the sum of lengths of the shortest common supersequence (SCS) of all sequences in each batch. The central problem is to determine the minimum total cost of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})$$\end{document}, among all partitions into k batches.ConclusionsWhen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} is the set of all balanced binary sequences of length 2n, we use combinatorial methods to obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_2(\mathcal {S})=7n-2$$\end{document} for any positive n, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3k+1)n-kC(k)\sqrt{n}<cost_k(\mathcal {S})\le (3k+1)n-\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -1$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document} and large n with C a constant depending on k. Similarly, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6kn-O(k\sqrt{n})\le cost_k(\mathcal {S}')\le 2(3k+1)n-2\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -2$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 2$$\end{document} and large n when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}'$$\end{document} is the set of all balanced DNA sequences of length 2n. Previously, the probabilistic model of this problem was studied by Makarychev et al. (IEEE Trans Inf Theory 68:7454–7470, 2022), where strings are unconstrained or without consecutive identical letters.

Background

DNA based data storage systems are particularly appealing owing to their high information capacity and durability. De novo DNA synthesis is a powerful biotechnology which can generate DNA sequences without pre-existing templates. The storage of digital data in synthetic DNA molecules has also received much attention [1–7], but its practical application of existing DNA synthesis technologies is currently severely limited due to its high cost and low throughput [8]. For example, the cost of synthesizing DNA oligonucleotides using dozens of building blocks can be up to hundreds to thousands of dollars.

Currently, the main methods for producing large quantities of DNA molecules is array-based DNA for synthesis [8]. Using this technique, DNA synthesizers can create large numbers of DNA strands in parallel, with each strand growing one DNA base at a time. This leads to a problem of finding a common supersequence for a given set of strands as an appropriate representative for the set. In order to solve the problem of a large number of DNA molecules exceeding the load of the synthesizer, we follow the methods in [9] that the DNA strands pool needs to be spilit into batches, with each batch size corresponding to the maximum load of the synthesizer. In DNA synthesis applications, the cost for a batch is proportional to the length of the common supersequence, and the total cost across all batches is proportional to the sum of the lengths of the common supersequences for all batches [9–11].

The mathematical formulation of this problem is as follows. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} be a set of sequences with equal length, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 2$$\end{document} be an integer. Partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} to k subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_i$$\end{document} (called batches), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [k]$$\end{document}, each of size almost \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{|\mathcal {S}|}{k}$$\end{document}. Denote this partition by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}. Define the cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost(\mathcal {S}_i)$$\end{document} to be the length of the Shortest Common Supersequence of all sequences in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_i$$\end{document}. Then the problem is to determine the minimum total cost among all partitions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document}, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S}):=\min _{\pi }\sum _{i=1}^kcost(\mathcal {S}_i)$$\end{document}.

To minimize the cost, one problem is to compute the shortest common supersequence of each batch, which is the well-known SCS problem. The SCS problem is known to be NP-hard and remains NP-hard under strict restrictions to the problem instances [11–13]. It has multiple applications in various areas, mainly concluding scheduling, data compression, query optimization, text-editing and DNA sequences assembly in computational biology [14]. Actually, one can apply SCS to estimate whether different species have the same ancestor. For example, consider a set of DNA strings from different species, if we simply assume the only mutations during evolutionary process is the lost of the DNA in the embryo, then the embryonic DNA string must be the common supersequence of the given DNA strings.

Let S be a set of sequences, we say a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}$$\end{document} is a common supersequence of S if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}$$\end{document} is a supersequence of all sequences in S. The length of the shortest common supersequence (SCS) of S is called the cost of S, denoted by cost(S). Given a positive integer k, a k-partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document} of S is a partition of S into k almost equal-sized parts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [k]$$\end{document}, and each of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lfloor |S|/k\rfloor $$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lceil |S|/k\rceil $$\end{document}.
