\begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_1=\dddot{\varvec{s}}_{x,n}\dddot{\varvec{s}}_{m,y}=(0^21)^{0.5n-4.5}0^310^310^3|(10)^n.$$\end{document} We calculate the first case that n is even, when n is odd, the calculation is similar. By Theorem 2.1 and Lemma 2.4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} |B(\varvec{s}_1)|\le&|B((0^21)^{0.5n}0^2(10)^{n-2})|\\ \le&\sum _{\begin{array}{c} 0\le l_1\le 0.5n \\ 0\le l_2\le n \end{array}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}l_1+l_2-0.5n-1\\ 2l_1+l_2-n-1\end{array}}\right) 2^{l_1+1}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-l_1\\ 2n-l_1-l_2-(n-2)\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n-l_2\\ 2n-l_1-l_2-(n-2)\end{array}}\right) \\ \le&\sum _{1\le l_1\le 0.5n,1\le l_2\le n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}l_1+l_2-0.5n-1\\ 2l_1+l_2-n-1\end{array}}\right) 2^{l_1+1}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n-l_1-l_2\\ n-l_2\end{array}}\right) \\ \le&\sum _{1\le l_1\le 0.5n}n\left( {\begin{array}{c}1.5n-1\\ 2l_1-1\end{array}}\right) 2^{l_1+1}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document} Actually we can show function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(l_1):=\left( {\begin{array}{c}1.5n-1\\ 2l_1-1\end{array}}\right) 2^{l_1+1}$$\end{document} is a function that increases first and then decreases of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1$$\end{document}. So when n is sufficiently large, we estimate the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(l_1)$$\end{document} by Stirling’s Formula [17]: 12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} n^{n+1/2}e^{-n+7/8}\le n!\le n^{n+1/2}e^{-n+1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} We give \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{l}$$\end{document} such that function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(l_1)$$\end{document} reaches its maximum value at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{l}=\frac{6-3\sqrt{2}}{4}n\thickapprox 0.44n$$\end{document}, then when n is sufficiently large, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{1\le l_1\le 0.5n}n\left( {\begin{array}{c}1.5n-1\\ 2l_1-1\end{array}}\right) 2^{l_1+1}\le 0.5n^2 G(\bar{l})\le 0.83n^{3/2} e^{-3/4}\times 3.75^n\\&\quad \le \frac{\sqrt{2} e^{-9/8}}{2\sqrt{n}}2^{2n}\le \frac{1}{2}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which contradicts the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\varvec{s}_1)|\ge \left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) /2$$\end{document}. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_1|=n+x+m+y> n+0.5n-2+n+n\ge 3.5n-1$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\ge n,y<m$$\end{document}, this case is almost the same as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x<n,y\ge m$$\end{document}. So we also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_1|\ge 3.5n-1$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\ge n,y<m$$\end{document}, this case is almost the same as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x<n,y\ge m$$\end{document}. So we also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_1|\ge 3.5n-1$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x<n,y<m$$\end{document}, and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}|<3.5n-1$$\end{document}, it is obvious that such supersequences cannot satisfy the quantity condition compared with the first case.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x<n,y<m$$\end{document}, and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}|<3.5n-1$$\end{document}, it is obvious that such supersequences cannot satisfy the quantity condition compared with the first case.

Proof of Lemma 2.6

In order to prove Lemma 2.6, we combine the idea of Theorem 2.1 and combinatorial methods to analyze the structure of the supersequences. Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {\widetilde{S}}$$\end{document} the bad sequences set.

Proof

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}=\varvec{s}_11^{l_1}0^{l_2}\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_1$$\end{document} ends with 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document} starts with 1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{l_1}0^{l_2}$$\end{document} is in the second segment in Eq. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(13)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1\ge 1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_2\ge 2$$\end{document}, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}'=\varvec{s}_11^{l_1-1}010^{l_2-1}\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} B(\varvec{s})\setminus B(\varvec{s}')=&\{\varvec{s}'_11^{l_1}0^{l_2}\varvec{s}'_2:\varvec{s}'_1\preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_1,\, \text {but}\, \varvec{s}'_11\not \preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_1;\\&\varvec{s}'_2\preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2,\, \text {but}\, 0\varvec{s}'_2\not \preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2, \varvec{s}'_1\varvec{s}'_2\in C(2n-l_1-l_2,n-l_1)\}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B(\varvec{s})\setminus B(\varvec{s}')\cap \mathcal {\widetilde{S}}=\{ 1^{wt(\varvec{s}_1)+l_1}0^{l_2+(n-l_2)}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} Notice that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Gamma _2:=B(\varvec{s})\setminus B(\varvec{s}')\cap \mathcal {\widetilde{S}}$$\end{document} is empty, we can replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}'$$\end{document}, and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _2$$\end{document} is not empty, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{1+(n-l_2)}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}\not \preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)$$\end{document} must be more than one since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{1+(n-l_2)}\preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}. Then consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{wt(\varvec{s}_1)+l_1+1}0^{n}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)-1}$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{n}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)-1}$$\end{document} must be in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document} since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_1$$\end{document} has only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$wt(\varvec{s}_1)$$\end{document} ones. Rewrite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underbrace{1\ldots 0\ldots 1\ldots 0}_{\#0=n-l_2}|\underbrace{\ldots 0\ldots 1\ldots }_{\#1\ge n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where the first segment has only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-l_2$$\end{document} zeros, and since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{n-l_2}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}\preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}, the number of 1 in the second segment is at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)$$\end{document}. Find the first 0 in the second segment, rewrite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_2$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underbrace{1\ldots 0\ldots 1\ldots 0}_{\#0=n-l_2}|1\ldots 10|\underbrace{\ldots 0\ldots 1\ldots }_{\#1< n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} then the number of 1 in the third segment is less than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)$$\end{document} since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{1+(n-l_2)}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)}\not \preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}. However, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{n}1^{n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)-1}\preccurlyeq \varvec{s}_2$$\end{document}, thus the number of 1 in the third segment must be exactly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-l_1-wt(\varvec{s}_1)-1$$\end{document}, and 15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} n-l_2+1\ge n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} So we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_2=1$$\end{document}, which is a contradiction with assumptions.

Binary batch optimization for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>3$$\end{document}

In this section, we focus on the batch optimization for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>3$$\end{document} and k is an absolutely constant, and n is sufficiently large. The upper bound is by a construction, and the lower bound is by calculating the gap between the optimal cost and a cost assuming each supersequence being ambi-alternating.

Upper bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})$$\end{document}

Theorem 2.5

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})\le (3k+1)n-\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -1$$\end{document}.

Proof

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} is partitioned into k equal batches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_1,\mathcal {S}_2,\ldots , \mathcal {S}_k$$\end{document} of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{|\mathcal {S}|}{k}$$\end{document}, and the common supersequences of each batch is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_1,\varvec{s}_2,\ldots ,\varvec{s}_k$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_1|\le |\varvec{s}_2|\le \ldots \le |\varvec{s}_k|$$\end{document} be alternating. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_i|$$\end{document} is the shortest length such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\varvec{s}_i)|\ge \frac{|\mathcal {S}|\times i}{k}$$\end{document}. Next we estimate the length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_i|$$\end{document}.

By Lemma 2.6, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_k|=4n-1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_{k/2}|=3n-1$$\end{document} if k is even. Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\varvec{s}}_l$$\end{document} is an alternating sequence of length l, then by Lemma 2.2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_l)|= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=2n-l/2+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) +1 & \text {if}\, l\, \text {is even} ,\\ \sum _{k=2n-l/2+1/2}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-1\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n+1\\ k\end{array}}\right) & \text {if}\, l\, \text {is odd.}\\ \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_l)|+|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_{6n-2-l})|=\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}. If l is even, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_l)|+|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_{6n-2-l})|\\&\quad =\sum _{k=2n-l/2+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) +1+\sum _{k=2n-(6n-l-2)/2+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) +1\\&\quad =\sum _{k=2n-l/2+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n-k+1\end{array}}\right) +\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 0\end{array}}\right) +\sum _{k=l/2-n+2}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) +\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 0\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n\end{array}}\right) \\&\quad =\sum _{k=2n-l/2+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n-k+1\end{array}}\right) +\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 0\end{array}}\right) +\sum _{k=2}^{2n-l/2}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n-k+1\end{array}}\right) +\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 0\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n\end{array}}\right) \\&\quad =\sum _{k=1}^{n+1}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ n-k+1\end{array}}\right) =\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This conclusion also holds for odd l. Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}_i,i<\lceil k/2\rceil $$\end{document} satisfies both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\varvec{s}_i)|\ge \frac{|\mathcal {S}|\times i}{k}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\dot{\varvec{s}}_{|\varvec{s}_i|-1})|<\frac{|\mathcal {S}|\times i}{k}$$\end{document}. So by the calculation above, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\dot{\varvec{x}}_{6n-2-(|\varvec{s}_i|-1)})|> \frac{|\mathcal {S}|\times (k-i)}{k}$$\end{document}. Thus we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\varvec{s}_{k-i}|\le 6n-2-(|\varvec{s}_i|-1) $$\end{document}, which is17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\varvec{s}_i|+|\varvec{s}_{k-i}|\le 6n-1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So by some simple calculations, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})\le (3k+1)n-\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -1$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}

Lower bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})$$\end{document}

The precise lower bound is a little bit complicated because SCS is an NP-hard problem. In other words, even if we find an optimal batch, we cannot easily give its cost. In this subsection, we apply the same methods as that in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=2$$\end{document}, and give a lower bound.

Lemma 2.7

When n is sufficiently large, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\varvec{s}}_l\ge |\mathcal {S}|/k$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l\ge 3n-C(k)\sqrt{n}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(k)=(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\frac{e^{3/8}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

Proof

For an alternating supersequence, we estimate the minimum length l such that it satisfy the quantity condition. If l is even, then18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 1+\sum _{m=2n-\frac{l}{2}+1}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \ge \frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) }{k} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{m=2n-\frac{(l-2)}{2}}^{n}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-1\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n+1\\ m\end{array}}\right) <\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) }{k}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}It is easy to see \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l<3n$$\end{document} since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document}. Next we estimate the length range of l by Stirling’s Formula when n is large enough. Recall that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} n^{n+1/2}e^{-n+7/8}\le n!\le n^{n+1/2}e^{-n+1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}By Corollary 2.1, we estimate the value from half of the whole balanced sequences, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}. ThenIf n is odd, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-1-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)(\frac{e^{-3/2}e^{-2(l'-1)^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-2$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+1-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.If n is even, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-2-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}$$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)(\frac{e^{3/8}e^{-2{l'}^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-4$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+2-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.If l is odd, we have the same conclusion. So we conclude that l has lower bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3n-C(k)\sqrt{n}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(k)=(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\frac{e^{3/8}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}

If n is odd, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-1-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)(\frac{e^{-3/2}e^{-2(l'-1)^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-2$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+1-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

If n is odd, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-1-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n+1}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+1)(\frac{e^{-3/2}e^{-2(l'-1)^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-2$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+1-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

If n is even, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-2-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}$$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)(\frac{e^{3/8}e^{-2{l'}^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-4$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+2-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

If n is even, suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=3n-2-l'$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (2n-\frac{l}{2}-\frac{n}{2}+1)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ 2n-\frac{l}{2}-1\end{array}}\right) ^2<\sum _{m=\frac{n}{2}}^{2n-\frac{l}{2}}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ m-1\end{array}}\right) \le (\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} which means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}+\frac{l'}{2}\end{array}}\right) ^2<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k})\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) +\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ \frac{n}{2}\end{array}}\right) }{2}$$\end{document}. Then by equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(12)$$\end{document} and some normal calculation, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{l'}{2}+2)(\frac{e^{3/8}e^{-2{l'}^2/n}}{n}2^{2n+2})<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{-9/8}}{\sqrt{n}}2^{2n+1/2}$$\end{document}, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l'<\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}-4$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l>3n+2-\frac{(1/2-1/k)e^{3/8}\sqrt{n}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

Theorem 2.6

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S})\ge (3k+1)n-kC(k)\sqrt{n}-2k$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(k)=(1/2-1/k)\frac{e^{3/8}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

Proof

By Lemma 2.6, if all bad sequences are in the same batch, then we can easily have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} cost_k(\mathcal {S})\ge 4n-1+(k-1)(3n-C(k)\sqrt{n})\ge (3k+1)n-kC(k)\sqrt{n}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(k)=(1/2-1/k)\frac{e^{3/8}}{2^{1/2}}$$\end{document}.

So we only need consider the shortest length of common supersequences of some batch which both satisfy containing at least one bad sequence and the quantity condition. The same methods as Case 3 in Theorem 2.4, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=0.5n-C(k)\sqrt{n}-k$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=m=n$$\end{document}, we can obtain the same conclusion for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>3$$\end{document}. We omit the similar process here, and directly give\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} cost_k(\mathcal {S})\ge (3k+1)n-kC(k)\sqrt{n}-2k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}

DNA batch optimization

In this work, we first provide a combinatorial approach for batch optimization of balanced binary sequences. And then estimate the upper and lower bounds for the minimum cost. For DNA sequences, we give part of the conclusion due to its computational complexity and space constraints.

Theorem 2.7

Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}'$$\end{document} is the set of all balanced DNA codewords of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n,n\ge 2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {S}'|=\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) 2^{2n}$$\end{document}. For all integers k, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_k(\mathcal {S}')\le 2(3k+1)n-2\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -2$$\end{document}.

Proof

We expand the binary SCS of each batch to DNA supersequence by replacing 0 with AT, and 1 with CG. Check that each batch contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) }{k}\times 2^{2n}=\frac{|\mathcal {S}'|}{k}$$\end{document} balanced DNA subsequences by mapping 0 to A or T, and 1 to C or G in each balanced binary subsequences. And these DNA subsequences in each batch are all distinct since their corresponding binary forms are different. Thus we give a construction to confirm the upper bound. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}

Results

In this paper, we consider strict balanced sequences. That is, we consider sequences of length 2n, where the number of 1’s is n for binary words, and the number of G’s and C’s is n for DNA codes. We first consider the binary sequences, then expand the results to the DNA sequences. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} be the set of all binary balanced sequences of length 2n, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}'$$\end{document} be the set of all balanced DNA sequences of length 2n. By using combinatorial method, we determine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_2(\mathcal {S})=7n-2$$\end{document} for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document}, we provide bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3k+1)n-kC(k)\sqrt{n}<cost_k(\mathcal {S})\le (3k+1)n-\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -1$$\end{document} when n is large, where C(k) is a constant depending on k. For DNA sequences, we give bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6kn-O(k\sqrt{n})\le cost_k(\mathcal {S}')\le 2(3k+1)n-2\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -2$$\end{document}. Note that, the gap between upper and lower bounds for binary balanced sequences is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}.

Discussion

In this paper, we study this problem in a combinatorial point of view. We restrict our sequences to balanced sequences, which is a key property required in DNA storage. Balanced DNA sequences (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$50\pm \epsilon \%$$\end{document} GC-content) assure similar thermodynamic characteristics, ensure all hybridization operations take place in parallel, and lead to very rare hybridization errors [1, 18].

The limitations of this study are as follows: when the set consists of balanced sequences of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n$$\end{document}, if it is partitioned into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 2$$\end{document} batches, the optimal batching strategy and the minimum total cost can be accurately obtained through combinatorial mathematics. However, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document} is sufficiently large, due to the heightened requirements for sequence distribution and the NP-hard characteristic of the SCS problem, it is challenging for existing methods to construct an effective partitioning and calculate the length of the SCS.

Conclusions

In this work, we use combinatorial methods to obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$cost_2(\mathcal {S})=7n-2$$\end{document} for any positive n, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3k+1)n-kC\sqrt{n}<cost_k(\mathcal {S})\le (3k+1)n-\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -1$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 3$$\end{document} and large n with C a constant depending on k, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}$$\end{document} is the set of all balanced binary sequences of length 2n. Similarly, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6kn-O(k\sqrt{n})\le cost_k(\mathcal {S}')\le 2(3k+1)n-2\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -2$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 2$$\end{document} and large n when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}'$$\end{document} is the set of all balanced DNA sequences of length 2n.

Actually, we can apply the same methods as that in Theorem 2.1, and obtain a similar conclusion that the maximum value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|B(\varvec{s})|$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{s}$$\end{document} is a DNA sequence, is attained by an alternating DNA sequence, which is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ATCG)^*$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\sigma (ATCG))^*$$\end{document} , where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ATCG)^*$$\end{document} means a concatenation of some (ATCG)s, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (ATCG)$$\end{document} means a permutation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{A,T,C,G\}$$\end{document}. Then by similar calculations regarding the basic number, we claim that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6kn-O(k\sqrt{n})\le cost_k(\mathcal {S}')\le 2(3k+1)n-2\lfloor \frac{k}{2}\rfloor -2$$\end{document}.

In future work, it is interesting to find a better lower bound, give an efficient algorithm of optimal batch and analyze the batch optimization problem of binary and DNA sequences with other constraints.
